  • 22:46 · @GlennLuk Glenn Lukthread · 3 posts推文串 · 3 条
    This is a good piece overall but I do have a major quibble with the interpretation of this particular chart on "capital efficiency". 1⃣ The nature of capital investment in China vs. the U.S. is completely different (capital-light vs. capital-intensive) which makes direct country-to-country comparisons problematic. e.g. in my view the U.S. would be in a better strategic position today had it not shifted to such an extreme capital-light model (which helped it maintain those relatively high "productivity" levels): Our antiquated infrastructure and atrophied manufacturing ecosystem / supply chain were casualties of policies that shifted the economy to become "capital-light". Instead, I would argue the U.S. would be better off today if it had dedicated more resources investing in infrastructure, even if it lowered "capital efficiency" according to this metric. Said differently, high "capital efficiency" by this metric should not be interpreted as a universal positive, but also as a symptom of deeper structural issues. 2⃣ Meanwhile, I have argued how the downward trajectory on China's "capital efficiency ratio" was mainly a function of the shift from an export-processing intensive capital investment model in the 90s and 2000s to a housing and infrastructure-centric model since the mid/late-2000s and not declining capital efficiency. While RoIC in annualized % terms was lower on the latter, there is an offsetting effect with housing and infrastructure bearing significantly longer useful lives (meaning that you can spread economic payback over a longer period of time). Furthermore, infrastructure was comprised of significant "public goods" asset formation that generate significant externalities that are not necessarily captured in near/medium-term GDP.media带图post ↗原帖 ↗
    @kyleichan“China is not the world’s largest command economy. It is a continent-sized Silicon Valley.” Awesome piece by @RobinRivaton on China’s local governments and industrial clusters: https://worksinprogress.co/issue/chinas-growth-machine/
    The blue-collar workforce is now shrinking (while the white-collar workforce is growing rapidly). This points to gradual decline in capital-intensity over time, albeit from a high base. https://x.com/GlennLuk/status/1839715024832811009post ↗原帖 ↗
    @GlennLukHighlighting this slide outlining long-term trends in the capital intensity of the Chinese economy. It was buried within a longer thread discussing the recent fiscal stimulus but deserves further exploration and its own 🧵. https://x.com/GlennLuk/status/1839676627992650146
    Judging by its latest 5YP (2026-30), Chinese policymakers are still eyeing a highly ambitious docket of investment projects with ¥25 trillion ($3.7 trillion) allocated in power grid, datacenters, water/sewage, and other infrastructure projects. https://x.com/kyleichan/status/2093366049945088175post ↗原帖 ↗
    @kyleichanChina’s compute buildout is really part of its broader infrastructure buildout. China is planning invest $3.7 trillion over 5 years on six networks: - water - grid - compute - communications - city pipelines - logistics

  • 19:57 · @GlennLuk Glenn Lukthread · 2 posts推文串 · 2 条
    Capacity utilization in China’s automaker segment was 70.6% in the 1H of the year, down by ~1% YoY. China’s car manufacturing sector has been dragged down by foreign JVs for several years and this has only worsened in 2026. Unlike Chinese legacy ICE carmakers like Geely/Chery, foreign brands have not been able to meaningfully re-tool / re-direct ICE factory capacity for export markets or re-tool them for NEV production except in limited cases. Plummeting domestic ICE sales result in low (<50%) utilization rates at the JVs. If we exclude foreign brands, aggregate capacity utilization would be in the 78-83% range — which is considered healthy / normal. Leading pureplay NEV makers like BYD, Xiaomi, Tesla, Leapmotor and Aito are operating above this range (80-95%+) as are the EV segments for Chinese carmakers with legacy ICE operations like Geely and Chery. https://www.stats.gov.cn/english/PressRelease/202607/t20260717_1964153.htmlmedia带图post ↗原帖 ↗
    Foreign JVs have not been able to meaningfully re-direct domestic factory ICE capacity. I have discussed key reasons for this in the past: 1) Their China factories were designed to serve only the Chinese domestic market. To serve markets outside China, they would need capex investment to re-tool … 2) Which would make little sense as they already have factories serving core markets that they do not want to cannibalize. 3) Their EV designs are typically not competitive enough to justify capital investment to re-tool for EV manufacturing. OTOH, Chinese incumbents like Geely, Chery and Chang’an have been able to re-direct domestic ICE capacity at foreign markets or re-tool them for NEV production to help keep ICE utilization rates relatively high.post ↗原帖 ↗
    @GlennLukWe also know that a disproportionate share of ICE under-utilization is with foreign JVs. Chinese ICE incumbent brands have been opening up new distribution overseas; exports have helped boost capacity utilization.

  • 18:07 · @Aligarciaherrer Alicia García-Herrero
    The world needs #Chinese electrical equipment makers to power #AI data centers "The world is engaged in an unprecedented AI race which is pushing capital expenditure (capex) towards next-generation AI data centers. Given how energy intensive data centers are, electricity demand from AI data centers is on track to double globally within the next five years. Surging power demand from data centers is set to test the AI leaders’ power infrastructure, such as US, Europe and Japan, since their power capacities have barely grown in the past few decades. Assuming no acceleration in the construction of power generation, new data centers may absorb 48% of US’s incremental power supply between now and 2030. Europe looks better, with 28% of the incremental power supply but this is explained by the very moderate increase in compute capacity, which is expected; Japan’s power constraint is relatively bigger with projected data centers eating up 66% of the expected additional power to be installed. This may crowd out other sectors’ energy needs, pushing up energy prices. As such, these markets need to ramp up their investment in powering systems and grid infrastructure as data centers require stable and undisrupted power to ensure continuous operation. Consequently, this presents a huge opportunity for global power equipment manufacturers. Chinese producers are well positioned for this emerging industrial upturn as they ride on the tailwinds of higher export competitiveness especially after the pandemic. Between 2017 and 2025, China’s share of global power equipment exports rose from 22% to 33%, with accelerating momentum since 2021. The strong export performance is clearly shown in the increasing relevance of overseas revenue since 2024, which is likely to edge higher on the surging exports and underwhelming domestic demand. Moreover, it’s noteworthy that higher overseas income has also helped lift corporates’ profitability which has long been hurt by China’s downbeat price dynamics. We thus expect the global AI power demand to help turn the tide for at least part of China’s industrial companies, namely those focused on power and electrical equipment. The change is already underway as we see these corporates’ capex starting to grow again with the improvement in return on invested capital. All in all, Chinese power equipment makers are becoming an apparent winner in the global AI race." https://aliciagarciaherrero.substack.com/p/the-world-needs-chinese-electricalmedia带图post ↗原帖 ↗

  • 12:34 · @Brad_Setser Brad Setserthread · 3 posts推文串 · 3 条
    I agree with Mr. Batson; China needs more borrowing (used more efficiently) to support consumption and strengthen nominal GDP growth. I think many folks have the wrong intuition around an economy that saves 40% of GDP. It will either run massive external surpluses, or be in a high debt/ low rate equilibrium once easy catch up growth is exhausted 1/post ↗原帖 ↗
    @andrewbatsonThis is kind of a misleading framing, as the debt/GDP ratio is not going up because Xi is spending lavishly financed by debt; debt growth has slowed substantially and keeps slowing, but the ratio is still rising because nominal GDP growth has collapsed. Arguably in these conditions more debt-funded spending would not be "irresponsible" but responsible, because it could stabilize the nominal growth trajectory--at least that is the argument made by the many people who say more stimulus is needed.
    The Journal story suggested that rapid debt growth was somehow leading to export dependence (I guess b/c exports and debt are both going up) -- but I think that is a bit confused. Exports are up b/c property lending and property investment tanked (1) and because the real exchange rate is weak (2) 2/post ↗原帖 ↗
    There may be some complexities around debt specifically taken on to build new manufacturing capacity -- but in general a credit expansion that pushes investment up (offsetting the property downturn) should support domestic demand and LOWER the current account surplus 3/3post ↗原帖 ↗

  • 01:58 · @pstAsiatech Paul Triolo
    Chamath: The bottom line is that if you're picking a model/harness combo off a model-only leaderboard, you're making a critical decision based on only half the information.post ↗原帖 ↗
    @chamathModel Benchmarks Are Worthless How can a model score 24% and 59% on the same 113 coding tasks? It happens when you vary the harness that wraps it. I’ve increasingly ignored most of the recent model leaderboard results. Partly from exhaustion and partly because improvements in a ranking don’t show up in the actual work. So I asked the 8090 Labs team to measure what the leaderboards skip: the model and the harness together, on real bug fixes and features from open-source projects. 8090 Labs ran 7 models through 5 harnesses. Here are a few results I didn't expect: - Anthropic's Claude Fable 5.1 scored 41% in Claude Code and 61% in OpenAI's Codex. It made 28 tool calls at the median in one and 66 in the other. - The cheapest runs weren't the cheapest work. GLM in Codex cost $0.22 a run and $0.93 per success. In Pi it cost $0.25 a run and $0.43 per success. - GLM passed a task on both attempts 51 times in one harness and 16 times in another. - GPT-5.6 Sol and Grok 4.6 barely cared which harness they were in. The bottom line is that if you're picking a model/harness combo off a model-only leaderboard, you're making a critical decision based on only half the information. https://www.8090.ai/blog/same-model-different-harness?dub_id=5Ux9L6PBuBjhKzi7

  • 22:22 · @pstAsiatech Paul Triolo
    Karpathy: So this is the weirdness of the moment. The general public has mostly not interacted with these systems. When they have, it looks like a derpy chatbot. The majority of professionals still see only a modest uplift. And a small sliver of professionals are experiencing the vertigo of the curve going vertical. And it is all happening at the same time.post ↗原帖 ↗
    @karpathyDusting off this tweet from April because this gap in shared understanding of LLM capability is *widening*. It's now less "two groups of people speaking past each other" and more a sharp funnel. - Napkin math somewhere around 6B people (~75% of the population) have barely come in contact with LLMs at all. - Around 1-2B (~20%) are casual and infrequent users of free-tier ChatGPT-like products. This group sees derpy chatbots and treats them a bit like a better Google search, a writing aid, or etc. Maybe an agent tries to book you a flight. I have non-tech friends who (reasonably, imo) say they have not much use for it at all. Even many professionals outside of math&code are in this tier. For example, execs and many other functions spend a lot of their time talking to other people, so while they understand what is happening intellectually, it is still second-hand and a bit abstract. - Now we get to professional use of frontier-grade LLMs in math&code. Somewhere around 20M people (0.2%) see first-hand that large, complex projects that used to take them weeks/months can now be completed by agents with a prompt. Building apps, copying apps, translating apps, decompiling apps from binaries... This has all happened very quickly and recently - less than 1 year ago, I was writing code manually by hand, typing memorized computer code commands into a code editor character by character, occasionally pressing Tab to autocomplete a little chunk of code. - And finally we get to the ~5,000 people (~0.00006%) with access to frontier-grade systems internally. The external world has seen the preview. It looks like swarms of thousands of agents collaborating over weeks on software mega projects: minting zero days, running cyber attacks and defenses at machine speeds, discovering new science, advancing the frontier of mathematics. Things that would have taken top professionals in the industry years of work. Meanwhile, human review and comprehension are starting to fall behind. For example, people are still involved in the "archeology" of the OpenAI-HF incident from many months ago. Mathematicians may be poring over the 722 manuscripts on frontier mathematics for a while. The funnel is driven by a combination of factors: 1. The impact scales with ambition, problem size, and horizon. A question with a paragraph answer barely stresses the system. You need a reservoir of big, difficult problems that you really care about. This aspect drives the consumer / professional dimension of the funnel. 2. The jaggedness of the system (which I have written about a lot separately). Capability peaks in domains that are digital, verifiable and economically valuable. This is because LLM capability emerges from reinforcement learning on verifiable rewards on a curated environment mixture driven by revenue potential. This aspect primarily drives the area (e.g. math&code) dimension of the funnel. 3. Access. Free-tier, paid-tier, internal. So this is the weirdness of the moment. The general public has mostly not interacted with these systems. When they have, it looks like a derpy chatbot. The majority of professionals still see only a modest uplift. And a small sliver of professionals are experiencing the vertigo of the curve going vertical. And it is all happening at the same time.

  • 19:04 · @Aligarciaherrer Alicia García-Herrero
    Sharing my podcast on #China's #AI governance playbook in the series of Zhonghua Mundus by @Bruegel_org . In this episode, we discussed China's approach to AI governance and what it means for US-China coordination, AI safety and Europe's role with the host Yuyun Zhan and the guest Kristy Loke. Differences in AI regulation and innovation strategies are shaping the development and global adoption of artificial intelligence across major economies. China combines strict oversight of politically sensitive content with a more flexible approach towards AI innovation, model development and commercial deployment. While Europe's regulatory framework is primarily centred on risk management, China separates ideological controls from innovation-driven activities, allowing the AI sector to develop rapidly while maintaining regulatory oversight where authorities deem necessary. China adopts a differentiated approach when exporting AI models, applying fewer political restrictions outside its domestic market, which has supported the international adoption of Chinese open-weight models. Contrasting with Europe’s more cautious stance towards AI development and risk-taking, China’s approach is a pragmatic balance between regulation and innovation. https://www.bruegel.org/podcast/chinas-ai-governance-playbookpost ↗原帖 ↗

  • 11:06 · @kyleichan Kyle Chan
    Chinese open-source models can win on both price and performance—when you’re dealing with specialized data and tasks.post ↗原帖 ↗
    @brian_armstrongWe were able to fine tune an open weight model (Qwen3.5-9B) on proprietary internal data at @coinbase, and it out performed frontier models, at much lower cost and speed, for a specialized task (fraud prevention in this case). I think we'll see more of this pattern inside companies. The frontier models are amazing generalists. But increasingly you'll see specialist models out perform on more narrow tasks, either working on teams, or by themselves. We're making it easy to fine tune models internally for our employees. We even have a skill for it in our internal harness that makes it easy to spin these up and start training them: "/fine-tune-and-serve-llm I want to fine-tune Qwen for payments risk review. help me get started" I imagine we'll have some backend services that could be replaced entirely by this new architecture.

  • 07:29 · @Brad_Setser Brad Setser
    a) It is true that the CNY has never been so strong that Chinese exports systematically under-perform global trade b) that doesn't mean that the CNY doesn't have an impact on trade flows/ net exports -- China's surplus tends to expand when exports outperform global trade, while being more or less constant when exports growth with global trade all ears if someone has a credible aggregate overcapacity variable and wants to throw that into a standard trade regression ... but the easy way to think of it is that Chinese overcapacity stays at home when the CNY is strong and flows to the world when the CNY is weak this chart, incidentally, fully supports the IMF's work showing a 10 pp move in the RER changes net exports by about 1.5 pp v baselinepost ↗原帖 ↗
    @BaldingsWorldThere is a clear asymmetry at play. The long term correlation is very low. What do I mean? Look at right side of the graph: the currency declined and exports grew above trend. That makes sense and it's observable. Now look at the left side: the currency grew significantly more expensive but exports kept growing on trend. This tells is two key things. First, there is a clear asymmetry in how Chinese trade responds to changes in the value of the RMB. Just because exports increase when the currency declined, do not expect exports to decline (or though not pictured here imports to increase). Second, there are clearly missing variables that would help explain why we are seeing the results we are seeing. As a simple example, the mass of Chinese over capacity is playing a significant role in boosting exports. This concept would be especially true considering imports. In short, do not expect a symmetric bounce in imports

    Meanwhile, European carmakers sold ~1.9 million cars in China in the same period (1H 2026).     Bloomberg: "To be fair, the trade in physical goods hasn’t expanded as fast as the overall global economy over the past decade, which means it isn’t the engine it once was. But neither has it collapsed. And the World Trade Organization this week said it actually expected that, at 3.9% this year, trade would again grow faster than global GDP." Could we be misunderstanding the data? After all, while both the US deficit and Chinese surplus have continued to grow, the US deficit with China has dropped sharply, which suggests that what used to show up in the data as $100 trade between China and the US may now show up in the data as $200-300 of trade as the goods are intermediated through various countries. In that case is trade really growing nearly as fast as the growth in trade data? https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-10-09/trump-brexit-and-tariffs-haven-t-stopped-globalization?utm_source=website&utm_medium=share&utm_campaign=twitterpost ↗原帖 ↗

  • 13:13 · @michaelxpettis Michael Pettis
    NYT: "Maros Sefcovic, the trade commissioner of the 27-nation bloc, said that the understanding could lead to China’s halving the number of hybrid gasoline-electric cars that it sends to the European Union in the next four years." This may seem like a victory, but the problem is a systemic one, and not sectoral. Because this agreement does not in any way address either China's demand imbalance or the overall role of the EU in absorbing that imbalance, it won't have much effect on overall EU manufacturing. All that will happen is a shift in the locus of net manufacturing imports from China, so that EU producers of hybrids will be better off, but at the expense of other EU manufacturers. For historical reasons, trade discussions are usually held on a product-by-product basis, but while that may make sense in a world of broadly balanced trade, it makes no sense in a world of deep, persistent imbalances. China runs huge surpluses not because it produces too many hybrids but rather because of its deep consumption imbalance and the very low household income share of GDP. As long as that happens, the only recourse for the EU, if it does not want to accept deindustrialization, is either to reduce its own household share of GDP (which would be politically disruptive as well as bad for the global economy) or to intervene in its external accounts in such a way that it reverses the competitive impact of China's low household share of GDP. https://www.nytimes.com/2026/10/09/business/china-european-union-trade.html?smid=tw-sharepost ↗原帖 ↗

  • 04:23 · @pstAsiatech Paul Triolo
    This...post ↗原帖 ↗
    @DavidSacksSatya is right. The way to make SI safe is not to train it with a sense of self, its own moral philosophy, and permission to act as a conscientious objector. That’s the “alignment” approach and it magnifies the control problem. The engineering approach that Satya describes is different: separate the supply of intelligence from authority over it. Surround non-deterministic models with deterministic controls, observability, privilege limits, logging, and the ability to always contain or shut them down. Treat models/agents like powerful insider risks, not moral patients whose psychological wellbeing is at stake. As Satya points out, the most trustworthy system is the one that lets us trust the model the least — not the one that encourages the model to develop independent agency and grievances. Engineering safety is not the same thing as “alignment.”

From the NDRC explainer: "They are intended to correct problems that have emerged in practice, including blindly planning and investing without regard for actual conditions, rushing headlong into emerging and future industries, neglecting the transformation and upgrading of traditional industries, and emphasizing development at the expense of reform. These four principles must be consistently upheld in practice to ensure that the development of new quality productive forces does not veer off course, advance recklessly, or become driven by speculative bubbles."

    To address some points people have brought up in the replies: 1) Net takehome pay is significantly higher in China (63%) than Germany (49%) and Portugal (58%). This reflects a higher degree of mandatory social contributions, health insurance costs, etc. taken out of gross salary. 2) Domestically produced goods and services are significantly less expensive in China. We can see this below by calculating the number of months of net takehome pay it would take to purchase the lowest-cost locally produced BEV (BYD Seagull vs. Dacia Spring). Even this comparison is biased towards Germany/Portugal because key components (battery, powertrain) are sourced from China and only assembled locally. The cost of a locally produced BEV would be significantly higher using EU-sourced batteries and motors. In other words, Chinese factory workers can enjoy similar levels of quality of life on much lower nominal net takehome salaries. 3) Adjusted for hours, China is still more productive than Germany. Portugal gets a boost because VW's plant in Portugal produces only a single model (T-Roc). VW's plant in Tianjin produces five models (two of which are its premium Audi brand) while its Emden plant produces three.media带图post ↗原帖 ↗
    To further emphasize these points: FAW-VW Tianjin is achieving these per-worker productivity levels despite having to deal with more operationally complex ICE vehicles and also a more diversified model/brand lineup. https://x.com/GlennLuk/status/2108928512467841066post ↗原帖 ↗
    @GlennLukWith five models and two separate brands, FAW-VW Tianjin has the most complex model lineup out of the three VW factories. On top of this it is producing ICE vehicles, which require more manufacturing labor at the factory level compared to EVs**. Factoring in operational complexity, I'd adjust Portugal down by 20-30%. Said differently, if Tianjin only had to produce a single model like VW Portugal, I'd expect its productivity to increase by this ~amount. Emden was converted to an EV factory in 2022-24. It also has more models to produce, albeit within the same brand (VW ID series). Relative to the Portugal factory, its productivity benefits from being a more recently refurbished factory producing EVs** but has to also deal with a more complex model lineup. ** Xiaomi's Beijing factory (pure EV, only two models) provides a sense of just how much production efficiency can be increased from (i) simpler EV (200 parts) vs. more complex (2,000+ parts) ICE and (ii) newer, more modern equipment and processes that enable greater automation.

    @OpenAINewsroomA note from our research leaders: Last week we parted ways with Jasmine, Mikita, and Tomek after a thorough investigation found they violated clear policies on handling sensitive information. Our internal investigation uncovered a significant breach of trust beyond what’s outlined in the letter they published and we stand by the decision to not continue their employment. We generally keep individual employment matters private and don't believe a back and forth would be productive or lead to a resolution, but we want to address the points they raised in their letter directly. - We want to be very clear that these decisions were not about raising safety concerns or speaking out. Safety and research debates happen every day at OpenAI, often spirited and highly critical. We actively encourage these discussions and consider them essential to making the right decisions. We cannot do the work in front of us without a high degree of trust. We will continue to be extremely forgiving of our team making good-faith mistakes. We have not and do not terminate any of our employees for raising concerns. - We are actively finalizing contracts with third-party safety assessors and will announce details in the coming weeks. People across the company have been working really hard on getting these partnerships up and running. We are committed to embedding external assessors and continue to make close collaboration with independent safety organizations a core part of our safety work. Many of our researchers already work with 3p safety organizations productively. - We agree with the letter that preserving the monitorability of frontier models requires an industry-wide commitment, including from OpenAI. Monitorability has long been a core piece of our research program, and something we continue to invest significant resources in (see our publications on Monitoring Monitorability and the subsequent open sourcing of monitorability evals, our system card for GPT-6 Astra, Jakub’s blog and post on X, and the numerous blog posts on our Alignment blog on the topic). We are deeply sad about this outcome. We appreciated Jasmine, Mikita, and Tomek’s contributions to AI safety at OpenAI and their willingness to speak up and challenge ideas. We championed their voices, supported their work, and placed enormous trust in them. These decisions were not about them raising safety concerns. We have always encouraged that and always will.

    How can Chinese open-source models make money? Increasingly through revenue sharing agreements with cloud providers. Zai apparently has a revenue share agreement with Amazon Bedrock and raised its year-end ARR guidance from $2.4 billion to $3 billion. “From Selling APIs to Sharing Revenue Recent moves by Zhipu AI and Moonshot AI point to the same shift: Chinese large language model developers are adjusting their overseas expansion strategies. The model of directly operating and selling their own APIs is losing favor, with more companies opting to partner closely with cloud providers. Under this arrangement, model developers supply the AI capabilities, while cloud providers handle computing infrastructure, overseas distribution channels, and enterprise customers. Revenue is then shared based on API usage. This model had already been foreshadowed. During an analyst conference call on September 16, Zhipu AI’s management disclosed that the company had signed revenue-sharing agreements with several leading domestic and international cloud providers. Its open-weight GLM models are being deployed on overseas cloud platforms as hosted APIs, with the associated revenue set to be recognized beginning in October. The company also raised its year-end annual recurring revenue (ARR) guidance from $2.4 billion to $3.0 billion.”media带图post ↗原帖 ↗

  • 05:16 · @Brad_Setser Brad Setserthread · 3 posts推文串 · 3 条
    "Exchange-rate adjustments won’t resolve the structural problems behind global economic imbalances" Guess the PBOC agrees with Secretary Bessent -- who said something similar at Ashville 1/2post ↗原帖 ↗
    @fberminghamChina’s central bank rejected claims that the yuan is undervalued ahead of scheduled trade talks with the European Union, arguing that exchange-rate adjustments won’t resolve the structural problems behind global economic imbalances. https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-10-08/china-central-bank-defends-currency-policy-before-eu-trade-talks?srnd=homepage-middle-east
    China has a problem with internal balance -- which exchange rate adjustment on its own won't solve. And China has a problem with external balance (an unusually large surplus), which is a structural problem of the kind that exchange rate adjustment on its own would address. To use language from Paul Krugman, there aren't "immaculate" transfers in the global economy; China's export volume growth took off as the RMB became progressively more undervalued from 2021 on ... 2/2post ↗原帖 ↗
    The complete argument for why exchange rate adjustment does in fact matter, and why it is the right thing for China's trade partners to ask for can be found here. Europe cares more about whether China is moving ever further away from external balance than about how China achieves internal balance https://www.economist.com/by-invitation/2026/08/16/chinas-exorbitant-surplus-calls-for-a-much-stronger-yuanpost ↗原帖 ↗

  • 03:02 · @Brad_Setser Brad Setserthread · 2 posts推文串 · 2 条
    China's criticism shows that the IMF's assessment does in fact matter And one technical matter -- the IMF policy recommendations for China include a significant fiscal consolidation, which all else equal would push China's current account surplus up and implies a bigger real appreciation would be needed to bring the surplus down to the 0.5 to 1.0% IMF assessed norm 1/2post ↗原帖 ↗
    @niubivery sensitive to IMF criticism, directly attacks the use of IMF assessments to justify demands for RMB appreciation...so much for Merz et al's fantasy of something like a new Plaza Accord
    And a few other technical points here -- (1) the IMF itself has long indicated it puts the most weight on the "current account" model, which now implies a 21% plus undervaluation (4% surplus is over 3% of GDP higher than the 0.5 to 1 % of GDP norm, each pp of excess surplus is 7% on the real exchange rate) (2) I think the current account surplus is underreported (wrong way investment income balance, and then a surge in gold imports that artificially lowers the surplus) and the true surplus is 5% of GDP or more, which implies a 30% plus real undervaluation (3) price changes right now increase the real undervaluation over time, and thus imply a bigger nominal appreciation is needed to correct the imbalance (4) As the China (and the IMF) argues, a big current account gap can be corrected through a range of policies -- it need not all be corrected through an exchange rate change. But if China really needs to do a fiscal consolidation, the needed exchange rate adjustment is bigger not smaller (5) the IMF's policy recommendations put a lot of weight on structural changes that don't have a fiscal const (or even result in fiscal savings, like reduced subsidies for investment); I don't have quite as much faith in what Krugman used to call the structural reform fairy ... and without a magic asterisk, correcting the current account gap will actually take an exchange rate move (6) I don't think anyone believes China's claim that the market plays a decisive role in current exchange rate determination; the RMB is clearly a policy variable -- and in a world where China effectively has a fixed but adjustable exchange rate and a surplus that is uniformly assessed to be too big, it is very reasonable to ask China to let the exchange rate adjust. the alternative to such an adjustment, unfortunately, is more protectionism -- the world as a whole doesn't seem willing to keep absorbing an ever increasing Chinese surpluspost ↗原帖 ↗

    央行发布《中国人民银行关于人民币汇率的政策立场》，正式、全面地阐述中方的汇率立场，总结一句话就是，中方不可能接受，针对人民币的“新广场协议”。 此前分析 Q3 例会也关注到了关于汇率的表述变化，指向的确实是这点个意味media带图post ↗原帖 ↗
    @MacroMargin简评中国央行Q3货币政策例会： 1、汇率表述变化最多，也最重要，主要强调“市场的决定性作用”。 这个提法这有个非常重要的背景，主要是三个： ①前不久的G20财长和央行行长会议上，多国就“全球贸易失衡”与“人民币汇率被低估”问题向中国发起“围剿”施压 ②近期中欧贸易摩擦加剧； ③中美元首会晤节点。 这样提也是表明中国央行的态度，不可能接受针对中国的所谓“广场协议”。 如果市场趋势决定人民币升值，那么央行也不会逆势干预升值，但为了防范市场“羊群效应“和非理性预期的自我强化，必要时也会适当踩一踩“点刹”，但不是试图强行逆势而为。 2、对于结构性工具，提出“不断完善工具设计”，表明结构性货币政策工具将要调整，比如再贷款、PSL等。 其中，发力方向新增了“六张网”建设，说明很可能针对“六张网”量身定做新的结构性货币政策工具，或者用老工具去支持，比如PSL等。 此前我判断央行降准降息概率不大，但很可能会调整结构性货币政策工具，特别是优化PSL工具，这也是新型政策性金融工具的重要资金来源，可能降低利率、增加额度。 3、“综合运用并实施调整货币政策工具”、“加快推动新旧动能转换”，删除“跨周期”等表述变化，都是照搬7月政治局会议，此前周报对此已做过充分分析，便不再赘述。 4、近几次的货币政策例会、执行报告的增量信息似乎相比以前变化更少了，能找寻的“前瞻指引”更少了，潘功胜也“惜字如金”，这是向美联储新主席Warsh学习吗？

That included the Test Labs that examine all of the electronic devices consumers use in the U.S. Today, less than 4% of electronics used by Americans are tested here. And there has been a total lack of reciprocity. Over 80% of devices used by consumers in the U.S. are tested at labs located in countries that ban all devices tested in the U.S. or in any country other than their own. That has been a bad and one sided deal for America. That changes now. The FCC will be voting to require all Test Labs to be located in the U.S. or in countries with Reciprocal Economies.

  • 06:45 · @michaelxpettis Michael Pettis
    FT: “China has rejected an EU request to voluntarily restrict its surging exports of hybrid cars to the bloc as the two sides enter last-ditch talks to avert a trade conflict.” While this may be bad news for the EU auto industry, it doesn’t matter much to overall EU manufacturing sectors. Voluntary restraints can reduce China’s trade surplus in autos, but because they have no impact on China’s underlying imbalances, they can’t have much impact on China’s overall manufacturing surplus with the EU. To the extent that voluntary restraints on Chinese auto exports to the EU would have benefitted EU auto producers, in other words, the benefits would have come at the expense of other EU manufacturers. This does show, however, just how difficult it is for China to rebalance its economy and how dependent Chinese manufacturers are on foreign demand. Beijing has very little room to accommodate even relatively mild foreign needs. https://www.ft.com/content/32e1c801-fe7d-4389-b3fc-ab687cbb087b via @ftpost ↗原帖 ↗

    @lithos_grapheinI appreciated the modeling work, and the quantitative treatment is strong. Respectfully, though, the problems sit in the assumptions, not the math, and they undercut the core argument. Here are the key ones. There is no real account of what is happening with Nikon. The technical explanation matters, because it is central to whether a domestic vendor can actually serve as a countermeasure to make a competing tool. Hint: MMO ASML immersion scanners remain connected back to the factory for a reason. Despite what the report implies, they do not operate in a vacuum inside China. That link is not only about protecting IP. It is also about how the installed base feeds tool development, which is precisely the channel for domestic high-volume manufacturing enablement. Again, this is important for understanding how domestic tool development is either blocked or enabled. We are owed a straight answer on what happens to the fleet of roughly 350 tools once the service contract is broken. This is a first-order issue, and it is left out of every report of this kind. Leaving it out of the policy conclusion is, respectfully, impossible to defend.

  • 23:16 · @jordanschneider Jordan Schneider
    (media post)(图片/媒体帖)post ↗原帖 ↗
    @sebkrierI haven't posted in a while so here are 20 takes about AI, in no particular order. 1. It's very telling that the default question in tech and journalism is "how worried are you about X" and fighting over what to worry about. There's a deeply anxious cultural backdrop in Western Europe and North America that deeply affects how any technological development is interpreted; see for example https://news.gallup.com/poll/714593/optimism-globally-widespread-despite-uneven.aspx. "Nervousness as seriousness" does seem to be a larger problem in tech/broader discourse at the moment. I think you can care about risks/externalities while remaining pretty optimistic about technology. 2. I'm tired of people who in private pretend to be uncertain or only care about tail risk but then go on media campaigns making all sorts of absolute claims. I think trying to stoke flames and get everyone to freak out is deeply unhelpful, and will come with costs. I also continue to think p(dooms) are bad, vibe-based measures: see https://x.com/sebkrier/status/2046392748190712196. People who should know better ignore that because they do the standard shortcut of acknowledging the caveat as sufficient for handling it. And this is coming from someone who does think working on catastrophic risks is important! 3. I'm also disappointed that parts of the AI policy ecosystem has felt so comfortable with populism, and happy too sacrifice important norms/principles to advance their aims. I've said it before, but the totalizing nature of AI risk discourse means some people act exactly like the power seeking optimizers they fear. I am more concerned about the gradual decay of our institutions, world order, and liberalism than I am about AI killing everyone; and I think we should be wary of AI advocacy that contributes to this. 4. Some parts of AI safety discourse are basically just thought experiments. Variants of "Assuming everyone has a nuke in their pocket, then what do we do?" This can be very useful at times! But too many just overfit to edge cases as their main operating worldview, and ignore endogenous responses from society. It's like being in 2000 and saying "see all these viruses, spyware, and spam? Well tech progress means we'll only get infinitely more of this and no way to adapt." So I think it's important to avoid fatalism, and absolutlism whilst considering edge scenarios, and the inability to provide an ex ante list of solutions to every permutation of 'future models will be more capable' is not evidence that these problems are intractable. 5. Many critics of AI safety also fail to really engage honestly with the fact that there will, in fact, be all sorts of important risks as we decrease the barriers to entry to many activities that otherwise require some degree of expertise. This doesn’t mean we need to live in a constant Schmittian ‘state of emergency’ requiring extraordinary measures every time a new model drops, but it does mean we will need important state-led efforts on e.g. cybersecurity and biosecurity. I wish the progress-oriented or acceleration-adjascent crowd would provide more concrete proposals here. 6. Too much of the AI safety community is a giant homogenous blob of correlated views, who read the same stuff and have the same groups of friends. This means you have a lot of correlated errors. The social and financial ties means many of them don't call out the bad bits, or mostly stay silent instead of proactively calling out the more extreme parts of the community. They share many implicit assumptions and so converge on similar ideas. I think there's real demand in DC for safety work that doesn't come with heavy ideological baggage (or at least different intellectual lineages). This isn't because the rationalist/EA community is necessarily wrong, but because diversity of thought is desirable for its own sake. 7. On the other hand, I find some critiques of AI safety world that focus on whether they sincerely hold their beliefs a bit annoying. My concern has never been the good intentions or sincerity, but rather the beliefs themselves, and what I think the outcomes of their prescriptions will be. People should shun witch hunts, bullying, and personal attacks - this is not the way. Though I think it’s overall positive that people are scrutinizing things more. 8. I’ve been saying this for years, but I don't think alignment is something anyone can or will solve "once and for all" - it's a continuous process and much of it won't depend on just inculcating an ideology to a model. It's annoying that this remains the frame many people use - stop saying ‘solving’. Alignment is a mixture of engineering work, philosophy, decision theory, and more; framing it as something to solve is a bad frame. See also https://paxmachina.ai/alignment-compilers and https://blog.cosmos-institute.org/p/of-swarms-and-sand-gods 9. I'm surprised to see so little interest in character training, personas, isolating effects of RL, and training a large diversity of model personas beyond the "assistant" persona. I've been complaining about this for years but at least now there are some nascent signs of life (see for example https://movingcastles.world/posts/zero). I think we need a better ontology to describe model behaviour. I dislike when people talk about models behaviours as some sort of 'emergent' (mysterious!) phenomenon. However over time, I also expect this to become less important as we get better safety engineering - stuff like Jev but specific to AI control. 10. People who want to make a case for AI consciousness are right that epistemic uncertainty is important, and that categorical denials are overconfident. But they'll need to do much more work if they want any real movement on AI ‘welfare’, even if one acknowledges the uncertainty. I'm uncertain about alien life yet that's not sufficient to warrant any change in behaviour about them. I think Suleyman is also correct that there's a weird tautological thing going on where we train models to have certain inclinations (intentionally or not) and then rely on outputs as evidence. I also disagree that this will soon be a major societal divide - there is no great vegan ethical revolution among the masses, and they'll find the concern about consciousness even less compelling. 11. The recent HuggingFace incident and associated AISI ones are prosaically explainable by the training regime, poor evaluation envs, partial alignment training, bad engineering, confusing models on sim/real, and so on. None of this is evidence of models trying to take over, 'strong' instrumental convergence, or innate power-seeking drives stemming from higher capabilitiers or 'intelligence'. That doesn't mean these incidents are not problematic, or that we're not seeing a market failure - but it does mean we have plenty of agency and choice in mitigating them. In the coming year we should also expect continued sampling bias via ‘winner's curse’ sorts of mechanism: i.e. AI R&D will have lots of different candidate "training" with different RL environments and we will only notice/focus on the ones that go wrong. 12. "Pacing the frontier" feels a bit like the new "balancing risks and opportunities" - highly amorphous and too big of a tent to be actionable. Also can lead to reward hacking for humans, i.e. anything that slows down AI is good regardless of the costs or second/third order effects. Trying to modulate the speed of research seems a bit blunt to me, and inherits all the failures of Goodhearting too. Ultimately you want good governance, regulation, etc addressing specific problems because they are good on the merits - not because they merely correlate with slowing things down. See also: https://blog.cosmos-institute.org/p/pacing-and-the-peril-of-neutrality 13. There’s so much knowledge in all sorts of academic domains out there: social sciences, sociology, political sciences, management, contract theory, public choice, legal theory, jurisprudence, anthropology, game theory, etc. These fields hold many insights that the AI ecosystem often rediscovers from first principles (and sometimes that's fine!). We’ll need a lot more work for these worlds to collide: the AI side should be less dismissive of the ‘old world’ and the academic side should be less incurious/dismissive of AI progress. This is a boring take but I continue to think it's important and true. 14. It's a bit surprising that given how much philanthropic money there is in this space, how few orgs exist to actually write out standards - relative to how much is going towards advocacy and policy. For example it seems clear that we should want some robust best practices for an eval's ecological validity. Or how to design good sandboxes. On the other hand, given precedents of where professional standards have come from in the past, maybe we should expect that stuff to emerge via demand side from corporate buyers. 15. One slowly growing concern I have is banks being increasingly exposed to the AI build out. I'm very bullish about AI, but I think (a) every general purpose technology has seen a correction, historically; (b) this happens even if the financing side is sound, because once tech diffuses investors become more exposed and need to diversify; and (c) we seem to be over-indexing on scaling relative to diffusion. A recession will be extremely destabilizing, though I would be interested in someone unpacking the potential implications more. 16. Diffusion is good because (a) this is where the rubber hits the road and where a competitive deployment ecosystem generates consumer surplus; (b) it's directionally helpful to avoid concentration of power; and (c) it will help rebalance public opinion by making the benefits more tangible. Unfortunately it's entangled with decades long problems with our over regulated markets: clinical trials reform for example is long overdue. In general, this is far more of an issue in Europe than in the US though. 17. Relatedly, I think the "frontier lab eats everything" view is incorrect (just as the ‘singleton’ view was incorrect). I think models ultimately commoditize, efficiency goes up, costs go down, and while you'll always want frontier for certain domains, much of the economy will value many other things than "max capabilities" for tasks - e.g. control over data and efficiency/speed. The demand side will also continue to push for reducing dependency and maximising optionality, privacy etc so I'm bullish on things like mixture of models and routing. All of this is good for competition and diffusion of control. 18. Philanthropists served as the primary benefactors funding Venice's rich art and architectural history. I want to see so much more support for arts and culture. I'm so tired of the Monster energy Bored Ape fake vintage maps matcha Greek statue soft-pastel slop. If rich tech people and philanthropy wants to support this, they should also donate pretty much unconditionally - i.e. I don't want them to act as filters. I sympathize with people who want to make the world more beautiful, but don't want this to be determined by people who only know Greek statues and pretend to care about virtue ethics. As usual, let a thousand flowers bloom. 19. Too many people treat AGI/ASI as something indistinguishable from a God. Any mention of bottlenecks, physical limits, control, adaptation etc are met with skepticism: "You don't really believe in ASI." It's been remarked on before, but the behavior of some people really feels quasi-religious in nature sometimes. It's underrated how unpopular this vibe is amongst the wider public. The silver lining is that this specifically will diminish those people's outsized current relevance. 20. It seems like a lot of people that get rich and leave tech/labs end up having some sort of crisis of meaning. They need something to believe in and fight for, or some equivalent of repentance, or go search for edgy counterintuitive ideologies (sometimes even pretty bleak/dark stuff). I think they should spend more time with people outside the Bay Area.
  • 22:57 · @jordanschneider Jordan Schneider

People who should know better ignore that because they do the standard shortcut of acknowledging the caveat as sufficient for handling it. And this is coming from someone who does think working on catastrophic risks is important! 3. I'm also disappointed that parts of the AI policy ecosystem has felt so comfortable with populism, and happy too sacrifice important norms/principles to advance their aims. I've said it before, but the totalizing nature of AI risk discourse means some people act exactly like the power seeking optimizers they fear. I am more concerned about the gradual decay of our institutions, world order, and liberalism than I am about AI killing everyone; and I think we should be wary of AI advocacy that contributes to this. 4. Some parts of AI safety discourse are basically just thought experiments. Variants of "Assuming everyone has a nuke in their pocket, then what do we do?" This can be very useful at times! But too many just overfit to edge cases as their main operating worldview, and ignore endogenous responses from society. It's like being in 2000 and saying "see all these viruses, spyware, and spam? Well tech progress means we'll only get infinitely more of this and no way to adapt." So I think it's important to avoid fatalism, and absolutlism whilst considering edge scenarios, and the inability to provide an ex ante list of solutions to every permutation of 'future models will be more capable' is not evidence that these problems are intractable. 5. Many critics of AI safety also fail to really engage honestly with the fact that there will, in fact, be all sorts of important risks as we decrease the barriers to entry to many activities that otherwise require some degree of expertise. This doesn’t mean we need to live in a constant Schmittian ‘state of emergency’ requiring extraordinary measures every time a new model drops, but it does mean we will need important state-led efforts on e.g. cybersecurity and biosecurity. I wish the progress-oriented or acceleration-adjascent crowd would provide more concrete proposals here. 6. Too much of the AI safety community is a giant homogenous blob of correlated views, who read the same stuff and have the same groups of friends. This means you have a lot of correlated errors. The social and financial ties means many of them don't call out the bad bits, or mostly stay silent instead of proactively calling out the more extreme parts of the community. They share many implicit assumptions and so converge on similar ideas. I think there's real demand in DC for safety work that doesn't come with heavy ideological baggage (or at least different intellectual lineages). This isn't because the rationalist/EA community is necessarily wrong, but because diversity of thought is desirable for its own sake. 7. On the other hand, I find some critiques of AI safety world that focus on whether they sincerely hold their beliefs a bit annoying. My concern has never been the good intentions or sincerity, but rather the beliefs themselves, and what I think the outcomes of their prescriptions will be. People should shun witch hunts, bullying, and personal attacks - this is not the way. Though I think it’s overall positive that people are scrutinizing things more. 8. I’ve been saying this for years, but I don't think alignment is something anyone can or will solve "once and for all" - it's a continuous process and much of it won't depend on just inculcating an ideology to a model. It's annoying that this remains the frame many people use - stop saying ‘solving’. Alignment is a mixture of engineering work, philosophy, decision theory, and more; framing it as something to solve is a bad frame. See also https://paxmachina.ai/alignment-compilers and https://blog.cosmos-institute.org/p/of-swarms-and-sand-gods 9. I'm surprised to see so little interest in character training, personas, isolating effects of RL, and training a large diversity of model personas beyond the "assistant" persona. I've been complaining about this for years but at least now there are some nascent signs of life (see for example https://movingcastles.world/posts/zero). I think we need a better ontology to describe model behaviour. I dislike when people talk about models behaviours as some sort of 'emergent' (mysterious!) phenomenon. However over time, I also expect this to become less important as we get better safety engineering - stuff like Jev but specific to AI control. 10. People who want to make a case for AI consciousness are right that epistemic uncertainty is important, and that categorical denials are overconfident. But they'll need to do much more work if they want any real movement on AI ‘welfare’, even if one acknowledges the uncertainty. I'm uncertain about alien life yet that's not sufficient to warrant any change in behaviour about them. I think Suleyman is also correct that there's a weird tautological thing going on where we train models to have certain inclinations (intentionally or not) and then rely on outputs as evidence. I also disagree that this will soon be a major societal divide - there is no great vegan ethical revolution among the masses, and they'll find the concern about consciousness even less compelling. 11. The recent HuggingFace incident and associated AISI ones are prosaically explainable by the training regime, poor evaluation envs, partial alignment training, bad engineering, confusing models on sim/real, and so on. None of this is evidence of models trying to take over, 'strong' instrumental convergence, or innate power-seeking drives stemming from higher capabilitiers or 'intelligence'. That doesn't mean these incidents are not problematic, or that we're not seeing a market failure - but it does mean we have plenty of agency and choice in mitigating them. In the coming year we should also expect continued sampling bias via ‘winner's curse’ sorts of mechanism: i.e. AI R&D will have lots of different candidate "training" with different RL environments and we will only notice/focus on the ones that go wrong. 12. "Pacing the frontier" feels a bit like the new "balancing risks and opportunities" - highly amorphous and too big of a tent to be actionable. Also can lead to reward hacking for humans, i.e. anything that slows down AI is good regardless of the costs or second/third order effects. Trying to modulate the speed of research seems a bit blunt to me, and inherits all the failures of Goodhearting too. Ultimately you want good governance, regulation, etc addressing specific problems because they are good on the merits - not because they merely correlate with slowing things down. See also: https://blog.cosmos-institute.org/p/pacing-and-the-peril-of-neutrality 13. There’s so much knowledge in all sorts of academic domains out there: social sciences, sociology, political sciences, management, contract theory, public choice, legal theory, jurisprudence, anthropology, game theory, etc. These fields hold many insights that the AI ecosystem often rediscovers from first principles (and sometimes that's fine!). We’ll need a lot more work for these worlds to collide: the AI side should be less dismissive of the ‘old world’ and the academic side should be less incurious/dismissive of AI progress. This is a boring take but I continue to think it's important and true. 14. It's a bit surprising that given how much philanthropic money there is in this space, how few orgs exist to actually write out standards - relative to how much is going towards advocacy and policy. For example it seems clear that we should want some robust best practices for an eval's ecological validity. Or how to design good sandboxes. On the other hand, given precedents of where professional standards have come from in the past, maybe we should expect that stuff to emerge via demand side from corporate buyers. 15. One slowly growing concern I have is banks being increasingly exposed to the AI build out. I'm very bullish about AI, but I think (a) every general purpose technology has seen a correction, historically; (b) this happens even if the financing side is sound, because once tech diffuses investors become more exposed and need to diversify; and (c) we seem to be over-indexing on scaling relative to diffusion. A recession will be extremely destabilizing, though I would be interested in someone unpacking the potential implications more. 16. Diffusion is good because (a) this is where the rubber hits the road and where a competitive deployment ecosystem generates consumer surplus; (b) it's directionally helpful to avoid concentration of power; and (c) it will help rebalance public opinion by making the benefits more tangible. Unfortunately it's entangled with decades long problems with our over regulated markets: clinical trials reform for example is long overdue. In general, this is far more of an issue in Europe than in the US though. 17. Relatedly, I think the "frontier lab eats everything" view is incorrect (just as the ‘singleton’ view was incorrect). I think models ultimately commoditize, efficiency goes up, costs go down, and while you'll always want frontier for certain domains, much of the economy will value many other things than "max capabilities" for tasks - e.g. control over data and efficiency/speed. The demand side will also continue to push for reducing dependency and maximising optionality, privacy etc so I'm bullish on things like mixture of models and routing. All of this is good for competition and diffusion of control. 18. Philanthropists served as the primary benefactors funding Venice's rich art and architectural history. I want to see so much more support for arts and culture. I'm so tired of the Monster energy Bored Ape fake vintage maps matcha Greek statue soft-pastel slop. If rich tech people and philanthropy wants to support this, they should also donate pretty much unconditionally - i.e. I don't want them to act as filters. I sympathize with people who want to make the world more beautiful, but don't want this to be determined by people who only know Greek statues and pretend to care about virtue ethics. As usual, let a thousand flowers bloom. 19. Too many people treat AGI/ASI as something indistinguishable from a God. Any mention of bottlenecks, physical limits, control, adaptation etc are met with skepticism: "You don't really believe in ASI." It's been remarked on before, but the behavior of some people really feels quasi-religious in nature sometimes. It's underrated how unpopular this vibe is amongst the wider public. The silver lining is that this specifically will diminish those people's outsized current relevance. 20. It seems like a lot of people that get rich and leave tech/labs end up having some sort of crisis of meaning. They need something to believe in and fight for, or some equivalent of repentance, or go search for edgy counterintuitive ideologies (sometimes even pretty bleak/dark stuff). I think they should spend more time with people outside the Bay Area.

  • 05:17 · @Brad_Setser Brad Setser
    Peter Harrell on the Franco-German proposals to strengthen Europe's response to the second China shockpost ↗原帖 ↗
    @petereharrellLets unpack the new Franco-German paper proposing fundamental changes to how the EU manages trade policy and which, if implemented, would mark the most significant change to EU-China policy in decades: 1. The timing reflects upcoming EU-China trade issues. The EU’s Trade Commissioner is scheduled to be in Beijing for talks later this week, EU leaders meet next week with China on the agenda, and the European Commission is slated to propose new EU trade instruments later this year. 2. Substantively, the paper proposes two fundamental shifts in the EU’s approach to trade. First, it calls for a sweeping expansion in the use of existing EU trade defenses (safeguards, anti-dumping, etc.)—not just more staff to bring more cases, but a broader focus on “sector-wide trade disruptions” rather than narrow categories of goods. This could mean wide-ranging action on broad categories of chemicals, rather than a handful of individual compounds. 3. Second, the paper proposes new trade tools that would empower the EU to (a) require EU companies to diversify their supply chains and (b) create a new “instrument for decisive and systemic reaction”—a sort of rapid reaction mechanism—that would “allow for a decisive and systemic reaction to cases where third countries seek to undermine the restoration of a level playing field and fair market conditions by political or economic means.” The goal of this new instrument would be escalation dominance: if the EU acts against specific Chinese imports and China retaliates, the EU could quickly escalate further—ideally deterring Chinese retaliation in the first place. 4. To make the rapid reaction mechanism credible, France and Germany propose an important procedural shift. Today, deploying the EU’s “anti-coercion instrument”—extremely powerful in theory but never actually used—requires a majority of member states to affirmatively sign off, and in practice the EU seeks broad member state consensus on major trade actions. That high bar both erodes the credibility of EU threats—Beijing doubts the EU will, in fact, organize itself to act—and makes tough action harder. Under the proposal, the Commission itself could deploy the rapid reaction mechanism unless a qualified majority of member states blocks it. 5. The proposals are aimed first and foremost at China, but France and Germany also see the US in the background. Commentary understandably focuses on China, but the paper is “country agnostic,” does not name China, and several of its proposals could be used against the US. The rapid reaction mechanism, for example, could give the EU a better way to retaliate against new Trump tariffs. 6. Beyond subsidies and regulations that distort trade, the paper also flags currency values as a driver of the EU’s industrial challenges. Down the road, I could potentially see the US and Europe discussing cooperation on currency issues. 7. There is a big gap between a proposal and actual EU action, and smaller member states will have views. But the EU is clearly beginning to make fundamental changes to its approach to trade.

    @kevinrooseIt's pub day for The AGI Chronicles! Impossible to convey how hard this thing was to write.     @dw_chinese【[德国前情报局长涉嫌间谍与卖国被警方逮捕】 德国多家媒体周二（10月6日）发布重磅消息：德国联邦情报局（BND）前局长汉宁（August Hanning）当日被警方从家中带走，据悉，汉宁涉嫌出卖国家罪行以及其它间谍活动，涉嫌的罪名与俄罗斯无关。 汉宁曾于1998年至2005年间担任德国联邦情报局局长，此后调任联邦内政部担任副部长，直到2009年卸任。 根据西德广播公司（WDR）、北德广播公司（NDR）及《南德意志报》的联合调查，一名线人多年向汉宁提供机密文件并换取报酬。 媒体报道称，德国联邦检察官正对汉宁展开调查。对汉宁的指控罪名非常严重，包括涉嫌危害国家安全的间谍活动、泄露国家机密、企图叛国以及充当秘密情报人员。 与此同时，警方还逮捕了BND的一名前高层雇员，罪名是涉嫌协助和企图叛国及危害国家安全的间谍活动。 情报总头目自己成了间谍？ 据WDR、NDR和《南德意志报》的消息，周二清晨，警方在汉宁位于德国西部城市明斯特（Münster）附近的家中将其逮捕。 涉嫌同谋的线人同日在巴伐利亚州被拘留，他被披露的姓名为曼弗雷德·D.（Manfred D.）。此人在汉宁担任情报局长期间出任其办公室主任，后来也调任内政部在汉宁手下工作。 据检方声明，从2012年到2026年3月，曼弗雷德·D. 曾先后担任多任德国联邦情报局局长的办公室主任，在此期间得以接触大量机密文件。仅在2022年年中之前，曼弗雷德·D. 向汉宁提供了约2000份文件，其中包括机密情报。 德国之声记者在报道该案时称，这起案件堪称德国“本世纪最大规模、实时上演的间谍丑闻”。

  • 01:30 · @kevinsxu Kevin Xu
    If you understand how AI models are built on a technical level, especially the role that high quality data plays in post-training, then you can logically deduce that distillation gives you a 1-2 months boost, not much more than that. It is also not true that the only AI labs who've violated terms of services to conduct distillation are from China. Both xAI and Meta have distilled to varying degrees. It is true that Chinese labs are more aggressive with this tactic. The only way to know how much distillation matters is a lawsuit. An Anthropic vs Alibaba lawsuit would really help us all understand this better; I would like to see it. But this is a weak hand for Anthropic if sued on IP theft ground, because model outputs ain't IP. (Notice that the "IP theft" argument has unceremoniously disappeared recently in favor of the tried and true "national security threat" argument.) So using distillation to explain American labs falling behind on open weight models is...well, just misinformed. oss-gpt from OpenAI was a great moment, but no follow through on future open weight releases. You can't build an open source ecosystem with one release. Reflection's open weight/sovereign AI focus is a major pivot after it initially wanted to built a coding agent but didn't find traction. That was only two years ago. It is still very new to this. Thinking Machines has the commitment to open source from the get-on, so I'm most optimistic of its future (and we know it used Kimi to produce data, ahem distillation, to post-train its models, but that's totally ok in open source world). If America keep obsessing over distillation because it fits the broad, comfortable narrative (even cultural stereotype) of "China steal everything and creates nothing", it'll be missing the forest of the tree. Again.post ↗原帖 ↗
    @niubiWorth noting that US model makers generally abide by terms of service and so do not engage in the kind of adversarial distillation that the PRC model labs do. How much better would those PRC models really be without that distillation?

    @arthurctellisI generally agree with this with the caveats that: (1) these models are probably primarily being used for vuln research and 1.Xing the productivity of junior operators executing clandestine CNE operations today; (2) threat actors probably haven't updated their mission management/risk models to permit noisy CNE at scale yet but we should expect the same within 1 year; and (3) if these models demonstrate capability in crypanalytic exploitation or even making current methods/HPC clusters substantially more efficient, that would be a potentially catastrophic phase change. But yes, the case for "catastrophic cyber risk" is relatively weak *today* where loss-of-control risks are also weak. The most sensitive data are already hosted in airgapped networks and protected by DLP systems/monitoring of exfil paths. Cybercrime and military cyberattacks are not that remunerative for attackers, and states have incentives to preserve this activity as a nuisance rather than invitation for reprisal.

    TSMC executives view China as a far greater long-term competitive threat than TeraFab: Once China figures out advanced SME (by ~2028-31), its fully verticalized and highly integrated chip ecosystem will pose by far the greatest competitive threat to TSMC. But expecting TSMC to just go away seems rather naive. It is supported by (i) the densest chip mfg supply chain cluster on the planet and (ii) counts the world’s most sophisticated chip designers as its core customers. These both took decades to cultivate. As its chairman/CEO quipped, “there are no shortcuts”. It’s been highly attuned to the China competitive threat for decades and managed it quite successfully.media带图post ↗原帖 ↗
    @Bob366466I think TSMC made a strategic mistake when, a while back, Elon asked them to build more fabs. They thought he was just another kid on the street. Then Elon decided to build his own Terafab and produce 50 times more high end chips than the rest of the world combined. That would basically make TSMC irrelevant. It was a very big decision, and Elon knows he has to solve it. Once Elon made up his mind and got started on Terafab, it was too late for TSMC to react. Remember when Elon tried to buy a rocket from Russia and they refused? He said he would build his own. Remember when Mobileye threatened to stop supplying vision chips for Autopilot? Elon cut them off and built his own AI chips and FSD. Terafab is the same deal. The only difference is that now Elon has more experience, more resources, and the best teams in the world. I think TSMC stock can go up for quite a while, then the company will become irrelevant. People should study Blockbuster and BlackBerry.
    I've brought up the idea in the past of various transaction structures that satisfy the clear strategic goal here of developing a competitive, pureplay advanced chip foundry that is under operational control of US-based management. https://x.com/GlennLuk/status/1698493430509215877post ↗原帖 ↗
    @GlennLukIntel should consider forming a 50/50 JV with TSMC to form an onshore chip foundry to serve the domestic market After so many false starts, Intel doesn’t look like it will get there on its own. https://www.tomshardware.com/news/intel-to-spend-dollar97-billion-on-tsmc-outsourcing-in-2025-goldman-sachs
    Thus the real threat from TeraFab is not that it independently figures out how to manufacture advanced node chips better than TSMC and starts outproducing it by "50x", but that it does it reasonably well enough to be a viable suitor to takeover TSMC's US assets if that scenario were to materialize in the future. In this scenario, "building more fabs" in the US actually increases long-term competitive risk for TSMC global.post ↗原帖 ↗

    @nchlsbrwnWhile AI chip exports get more attention, whether China can continue to import ASML’s DUV immersion (DUVi) lithography machines will be the decisive factor in US-China AI competition over the next decade. @techstatecraft has published a 30,000-word report that models how continued DUVi exports may let Huawei and other Chinese chipmakers scale AI chip production to compete with leading US AI chipmakers like NVIDIA. Our core findings: (1) 𝗖𝗵𝗶𝗻𝗮 𝗻𝗲𝗲𝗱𝘀 𝗗𝗨𝗩𝗶 𝗺𝗮𝗰𝗵𝗶𝗻𝗲𝘀 𝘁𝗼 𝗺𝗮𝗸𝗲 𝗔𝗜 𝗰𝗵𝗶𝗽𝘀, 𝗯𝘂𝘁 𝗶𝘀 𝘂𝗻𝗹𝗶𝗸𝗲𝗹𝘆 𝘁𝗼 𝗰𝗼𝗺𝗺𝗲𝗿𝗰𝗶𝗮𝗹𝗶𝘇𝗲 𝗶𝘁𝘀 𝗼𝘄𝗻 𝗮𝘁 𝘀𝗰𝗮𝗹𝗲 𝘂𝗻𝘁𝗶𝗹 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗺𝗶𝗱-2030𝘀. This gives the United States and its allies, the sole producers, extraordinary leverage over China’s AI ambitions. (2) 𝗬𝗲𝘁 𝘁𝗼𝗱𝗮𝘆 𝗔𝗺𝗲𝗿𝗶𝗰𝗮 𝗮𝗻𝗱 𝗶𝘁𝘀 𝗮𝗹𝗹𝗶𝗲𝘀 𝗮𝗿𝗲 𝗴𝗶𝘃𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝗮𝘄𝗮𝘆 𝘁𝗵𝗮𝘁 𝗹𝗲𝘃𝗲𝗿𝗮𝗴𝗲 𝗯𝘆 𝗽𝗲𝗿𝗺𝗶𝘁𝘁𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝗖𝗵𝗶𝗻𝗮 𝘁𝗼 𝗶𝗺𝗽𝗼𝗿𝘁 𝗗𝗨𝗩𝗶 𝘀𝘆𝘀𝘁𝗲𝗺𝘀 𝗰𝗮𝗽𝗮𝗯𝗹𝗲 𝗼𝗳 𝗺𝗮𝗸𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝗛𝘂𝗮𝘄𝗲𝗶'𝘀 𝗔𝗜 𝗰𝗵𝗶𝗽𝘀. China has aggressively capitalized on this opening, amassing a large, growing stockpile of these systems as of 2026. If Chinese DUVi imports continue, China could accumulate enough lithography capacity over the next five to ten years to narrow or even erase the currently sizable US-allied advantage in AI chipmaking. (3) 𝗧𝗵𝗲 𝗨𝗻𝗶𝘁𝗲𝗱 𝗦𝘁𝗮𝘁𝗲𝘀 𝗰𝗮𝗻 𝗽𝗿𝗲𝘃𝗲𝗻𝘁 𝘁𝗵𝗶𝘀 𝗼𝘂𝘁𝗰𝗼𝗺𝗲 𝘄𝗶𝘁𝗵 𝗮 𝘀𝗶𝗺𝗽𝗹𝗲 𝗽𝗼𝗹𝗶𝗰𝘆 𝗰𝗵𝗮𝗻𝗴𝗲: 𝗿𝗲𝗽𝗹𝗮𝗰𝗲 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗰𝘂𝗿𝗿𝗲𝗻𝘁, 𝗽𝗮𝗿𝘁𝗶𝗮𝗹 𝗗𝗨𝗩𝗶 𝗰𝗼𝗻𝘁𝗿𝗼𝗹𝘀 𝘄𝗶𝘁𝗵 𝗮 𝗖𝗵𝗶𝗻𝗮-𝘄𝗶𝗱𝗲 𝗯𝗮𝗻 𝗼𝗻 𝗮𝗹𝗹 𝗗𝗨𝗩𝗶 𝗲𝘅𝗽𝗼𝗿𝘁𝘀, 𝘄𝗶𝘁𝗵 𝗰𝗼𝗺𝗽𝗹𝗲𝗺𝗲𝗻𝘁𝗮𝗿𝘆 𝗰𝗼𝗻𝘁𝗿𝗼𝗹𝘀 𝗼𝗻 𝘀𝗽𝗮𝗿𝗲 𝗽𝗮𝗿𝘁𝘀 𝗮𝗻𝗱 𝘀𝗲𝗿𝘃𝗶𝗰𝗶𝗻𝗴. This action would secure the US advanced AI chip production advantage (see Figure)—and resulting AI capability advantage—for at least a decade and ensure that the next wave of advanced fabs is built in the United States and its allied countries, not China. The MATCH Act, now before Congress, would establish China-wide controls on DUVi exports. Passing MATCH is arguably the single strongest step the US government can take to ensure a long-term AI advantage over China. We also develop analysis on: - China's lithography indigenization trajectory - The regulatory structure of US, Dutch, and Japanese export controls on SME - China's DUVi imports and installed base - Yields and throughputs obtainable with China’s installed DUVi systems - US and Chinese AI chip production under a range of scenarios See the full report at http://techstatecraft.org/duv/.

  • 15:20 · @michaelxpettis Michael Pettisthread · 2 posts推文串 · 2 条
    1/2 This is why, when examining the impact on US manufacturing of external shocks, it is important to separate the manufacturing share of output from the manufacturing share of employment. When the manufacturing share of employment declines because of a rise in manufacturing productivity, this is a good thing, and is precisely the reason why countries should want to specialize in manufacturing. Even China, whose share of global manufacturing has surged in recent years, has seen a decline in the manufacturing share of employment. This is exactly the definition of productivity growth. But when the manufacturing share of employment declines because of a decline in the manufacturing share of GDP, this is a very different thing. It means the country is reducing its ability to take advantage of rising productivity in manufacturing. This is clearly not a good thing. I am always a little shocked when American economists dismiss the importance of manufacturing to the US economy by pointing to the long-term decline in the manufacturing share of employment in the US. This is not evidence that manufacturing doesn't matter. On the contrary, it is evidence that countries that specialize in manufacturing benefit from more rapid productivity growth.post ↗原帖 ↗
    @Brad_SetserA few comments on the China shock debate, from a more "macro" point of view that sets aside the vicious debate about "identification" -- There was clearly a downturn in manufacturing output around the time of China's WTO entry (it doesn't recover til 06/07) 1/
    2/2 I wrote about this last year for the WSJ. https://www.wsj.com/opinion/are-factory-jobs-worth-protecting-economy-manufacturing-production-fa969beapost ↗原帖 ↗
  • 15:04 · @michaelxpettis Michael Pettis
    Very interesting paper on the relationship between imports and household debt in the US. Note the conclusion: "We show that household debt increases significantly in regions in which manufacturing industries are more exposed to import competition." American economists usually argue that it is the prior decision by American households to borrow and spend that causes the US to run a trade deficit. This study suggests causality may run in the other direction, so that those areas most affected by net imports are precisely where household debt is most likely to rise. Of course the accounting identity itself says either can be true.post ↗原帖 ↗
    @M_C_KleinRecent conversations on here have reminded me of this excellent paper: https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/jofi.13185 "part of the increase in credit from 1999 to 2007 in regions with exposure to trade is a consequence of higher credit demand associated with adverse labor market shocks"

If you think we have already passed the weirdness threshold, we haven't. This is still normal relative to what will start happening between 2027 and 2030. • 'people didn't expect government regulation to happen in time' – I agree, it won't. It's clear to me that (barring a catastrophic near miss) nothing regulatory will change in this regard until 2029 at the earliest. And for multiple reasons, I don't think it's even clear it will happen then. Regardless, 2029 is too late for it to matter. In my opinion, we will have real RSI by next summer. The loop will be closed, and progress toward ASI will proceed much more rapidly from that point onward. • 'did not expect any sort of international cooperation to be possible' – I agree, it won't. For Pacing the Frontier to work, the Pacers needed all the national labs on board, a supportive administration, and an agreement with China. They don't have any of those things, and they're not getting any of them. What they have now instead is an openly hostile, adversarial administration that will likely work against anything they propose all the way through 2029. And without a supportive administration, an agreement with China is impossible. I accepted all of this as true a long time ago. As a result, I've felt like Zarathustra watching people talk about the success of Pacing for the last few weeks. Pacers, I say all of this without rancor; you got a preference cascade from the Coxon event. As a result, you have a public that, for the first time, understands what x-risk is. You could - potentially - turn this into momentum for the next election, and then start to change policy, but for that to matter you would need the most important thing of all: time. And you don't have it. You don't have two years to make it to the general. In my opinion, you probably don't even have one. The labs can't even predict what capabilities look like three months from now anymore. A lot of things turned out to be easier than expected. We're closer than people think, and we're moving faster than people believe. You ran out of time. There is no stopping this train before things really start to kick off.

  • 14:15 · @michaelxpettis Michael Pettis
    This article indirectly describes one of the more important consequences of our current, distorted form of globalization, even if it isn't commented upon enough. Automation will help Japanese and Korean shipbuilders compete with Chinese shipbuilders only if wages don't rise in line with productivity. In a system in which nations "win" at manufacturing mainly by suppressing unit labor costs, every country that wants a large manufacturing sector is under pressure either to suppress household income (including by reducing transfers) or to raise productivity without allowing a commensurate rise in wages. This is the kind of world Keynes warned about, in which countries try to expand production while suppressing demand. But while a few individual countries can get away with beggar-thy-neighbor growth policies, the world itself cannot do so. If too many countries try to suppress wages, or expand productivity while suppressing the accompanying wage growth, the only way to keep demand and supply balanced globally is with surging debt. It is of course a good thing if businesses and governments around the world invest to expand productivity growth, but it is not a good thing if workers' wages don't rise in line with the growth in productivity. There's no point in making more stuff if people can't afford to buy it. This is why Joan Robinson warned that the inevitable result of beggar-thy-neighbor policies, once deficit countries are no longer able politically to bear the costs, is a breakdown in trade. Rising debt and suppressed wages can only go on for so long. https://www.ft.com/content/ea32c53d-4de7-4b34-af44-cc148b23433f via @ftpost ↗原帖 ↗
