  • 22:36 · @GlennLuk Glenn Lukthread · 3 posts推文串 · 3 条
    Data in this old 🧵below from 2023 is dated but provides a sturdy framework to think through how China's petroleum demand is evolving over time: Passenger/commercial vehicles (~40%): Split ~evenly between gasoline & diesel. Reduction in usage driven almost entirely by transport electrification: from peak (in ~2022-23), I forecast 60-65% of this goes away by 2035, driven by declining stock of passenger/commercial ICE vehicles. This is no longer a question of "if" but when. Policy tools like trade-in vouchers have been used to accelerate the transition and there is policy room to aggressively ramp, if seen as necessary. Air travel (~6-7%): This is kerosene / jet fuel. Unlike terrestrial transport, unlikely to be replaced by batteries anytime soon. Long-term reduction is from the rise of e-kerosene synthesized from renewable wind/solar, mainly in the northeast. China's investment in renewables-driven green hydrogen infrastructure and coal-to-liquids (CTL) are going to be play a prominent role in replacing jet fuel (refined from imported petroleum). Another indirect impact worth mentioning is the fully electrified HSR system, which absorbs significant demand for long-distance travel and suppresses jet fuel demand growth. Industrial & Petrochemical (~40%): Naphta, LPG, Lubricants, Petroleum Coke, Industrial Diesel. This one is tricky to analyze because a significant portion of this is ultimately driven by direct and indirect foreign demand (expressed in the trade surplus). There has been significant expansion of refining capacity, and China is now a net exporter of refined petroleum products. This means it is importing crude oil, refining it, and then re-exporting it to the tune of ~0.8M bpd (~5% of total petroleum demand). Further, there is indirect "embodied" oil in its vast manufacturing sector where energy is one component in a finished good that is exported abroad. High-level estimate that this accounts for another 13-17% of China's total petroleum demand. Thus, true domestic demand only accounts for ~half of this category. Direct refined fuel exports are easy to shut off; indeed we have seen Chinese policymakers put controls on this in recent weeks. The indirect "embodied" oil is tied to the manufacturing sector and much more disruptive to shut down. But domestic energy security is a key reason I see continued (if not accelerated) offshoring of export-oriented manufacturing, especially those that use significant petroleum byproducts like plastic. Construction / Infrastructure (~9%): Asphalt/Bitumen, diesel for heavy machinery. This is already in structural decline as China moves past peak greenfield construction into maintenance mode. Roads and highway building (which relies on asphalt**) peaked 4-5 years ago. ** Venezuela had been a key supplier of the heavy oil that is optimized for asphalt. Residential / Commercial (~4-5%): This is mainly LPG and heating oil for off-grid heating and commercial cooking. Baseline demand here is steady but this can ultimately be electrified over time. Bottom line: Ultimately China's strategic goal is to replace all imported petroleum. Currently, imported petroleum comprises ~70% of its total demand: ▪️ Current trajectory of Transport Electrification will shave of ~30-35% ▪️ Green Hydrogen and CTL another ~10-15% ▪️ ~5% by giving up its net refinery exports (already in process) ▪️ Another ~10-15% by offshoring oil/petrochemical-intensive manufactured goods exportspost ↗原帖 ↗
    @GlennLukSnapshot of China's current petroleum demand: Oil consumption in 2019: ▪️ Vehicles - Passenger/Commercial (41%) ▪️ Planes (6%) ▪️ Industry (16%) ▪️ Feedstock (18%) ▪️ Other (19%)
    On the scale of investment required to replace domestic jet fuel demand with e-kerosene synthesized from renewables: https://x.com/GlennLuk/status/1808175443461218400post ↗原帖 ↗
    @GlennLukTo replace all current kerosene demand, China would have to build ~112 of these plants. Assuming costs stay the same — which is quite conservative given how much key capital cost items like wind turbines have fallen in recent years — it would cost ~$165B to build this capacity.
    Re: asphalt and the Venezuela connection https://x.com/GlennLuk/status/2007516928479268937post ↗原帖 ↗
    @GlennLukAnother consideration are end-use derivatives that Venezuelan heavy oil is better-suited for, namely asphalt and diesel. Diesel we touch on above with China's transport electricification (specifically of commercial trucks) but we have not talked about asphalt. Asphalt's main use (~88%) in China is for paving roads. China has reached post-peak greenfield road construction and is moving into maintenace mode, which requires less asphalt. In the 15th FYP, asphalt demand is expected to fall from ~31 million tons in 2025 to ~27 million per year.

  • 07:54 · @kyleichan Kyle Chan
    This widely cited stat about China’s expected share of global manufacturing value-add is problematic and shouldn’t be used. Thanks @gdp1985 and @JonathonPSine for catching this.post ↗原帖 ↗
    @gdp1985People understandably cite this UNIDO chart, claiming China will account for 45% of the world’s manufacturing value added by 2030, but it’s wrong and shouldn’t be cited. It comes from a 2024 UNIDO paper, which @JKynge cites in his new @ChathamHouse report on China and European industry. (His report is worth reading. My issue is only with the UNIDO figure!) UNIDO says it took each country’s average manufacturing growth over 2010–19 and projected it forward from 2024. But if you do that with UNIDO’s own data, China reaches 38% in 2030. The published numbers only come out if you use 2000–19 growth instead, which assumes China’s 13% annual growth from the post-WTO boom suddenly resumes in the coming years (it won’t). This appears to be an error in the UNIDO report. The UNIDO chart also says “industrial production,” but its numbers match manufacturing value added. China’s manufacturing growth has since slowed to around 5%. If we apply each economy’s 2019–25 manufacturing growth rate, China reaches 35% by 2030. In current dollars, China’s share peaked at 30.5% in 2021 and was 28% in 2024 because of deflation and a weaker yuan. China’s manufacturing sector is the biggest in the world, and it’s still growing. But it won’t reach 45% of global manufacturing in 2030. That would require China’s manufacturing output to grow about 14% a year for five years. Even if the rest of the world’s manufacturing didn’t grow at all, China would need 11% a year. It hasn’t grown that fast in any year since 2011. h/t @JonathonPSine, who pointed out some of the UNIDO issues last year.

  • 21:50 · @GlennLuk Glenn Lukthread · 3 posts推文串 · 3 条
    China is investing more than ever in nominal ¥ terms, but equivalent construction labor hours have ~halved from the 2012-14 period, driven by labor unit costs ~tripling. Actual construction output (e.g. km of HSR built) has been able to keep up due to productivity gains from mechanization of the manufacturing process, with the bulk of productivity gains in the 2016-2022 period. This chart illustrates how technology-enabled productivity gains enable China to continue being productive even while its labor force starts to shrink.media带图post ↗原帖 ↗
    The recently opened XiKang line cost ~¥208M/km, so ~double GuiGuang. However, it was an even higher bridge/tunnel ratio (94%, with tunnels at 83%) and was designed at a 350 km/hr spec. This suggests major improvements in civil engineering in mountainous regions over the last dozen years. https://x.com/GlennLuk/status/2104985012403876127post ↗原帖 ↗
    @GlennLukFinally, the new 171 km Xi'an-Ankang line cuts through the Qinling Mountain range (>90% bridges + tunnels) and is part of an eventual high-capacity regional railway connecting Xi'an all the way down to Chongqing. The future extension down to Chongqing is still under construction (ETA: 2028) and once completed will connect Xi'an to Chongqing within 2.5 hours (from 5-6 hours today through indirect routes). For now, the line connects Ankang (urban population: 1.2M) to Xi'an and brings it within the <1 hour "commuter zone". The trip had previously taken 3-3.5 hours via conventional train on winding mountain routes.
    The learning rate for a mature category like railway civil engineering is of course much lower than emerging categories like renewables and electric vehicles over the past decade and AI today. But there are still incremental improvements to apply that can mitigate the effects of rising labor costs driven by workforce shrinkage (supply/demand for labor) and general increasing economic productivity throughout the economy.post ↗原帖 ↗

  • 05:44 · @vshih2 Victor Shih 史宗瀚
    “Could we, and should we, divert some of the massive investments in AI towards public health if what we want is better health and longer lives for Americans? “ something to ponder on….post ↗原帖 ↗
    @DAcemogluMITFourth question on AI. One of the great promises of AI is the discovery of new drugs and cures, so that we can live longer and healthier lives. If and when this becomes a reality, it would indeed be a big achievement. These potential health benefits are often invoked to justify current investments (and lack of regulations). Some even argue that slowing down AI would harm humanity by delaying these benefits. (See https://a16z.com/the-techno-optimist-manifesto/). This raises another uncomfortable question: can we justify rapid and large AI investments based on health benefits? Here is why I think this is an uncomfortable question. First, despite significant effort, AI has so far produced few gains in drug discovery or new cures. A fascinating new paper by Ryan Hill and Carolyn Stein documents significant (downstream) scientific work on proteins whose structures AlphaFold predicted. But the authors conclude “we find no evidence so far that more applied, early-stage drug development is targeting these proteins.” (See their paper here https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=6395041). This thought-provoking post by Daphne Koller explains the difficulties that AI is currently facing in drug discovery. Koller emphasizes that AI-based research is focused on the last layer of drug development (creating new molecular entities for already well-understood therapeutic modalities), rather than targeting new disease mechanisms (see https://deepphenotype.substack.com/p/drug-discovery-has-no-magic-wands). Understanding disease mechanisms is harder because there is much less data on it, and this endeavor requires more innovative approaches. It remains an open question whether current AI models are capable of doing this. Second and more importantly, if you want to improve life expectancy and health in the United States, there is plenty of low hanging fruit and diverting some of the huge investments going to AI for this purpose would likely do much more for health outcomes. The United States has the lowest life expectancy at birth among large rich economies. Americans live about five years less, in expectation, than the citizens of Switzerland, Sweden, Japan, Italy, South Korea and several other industrialized countries, and most of the gap comes from deaths before age of 70 due to chronic diseases, overdoses and other preventable causes (see, for example, https://www.healthsystemtracker.org/chart-collection/what-drives-differences-in-life-expectancy-between-the-u-s-and-comparable-countries/). These largely reflect failures in US public health: preventable problems, unhealthy diets, insufficient immunization rates, poor access to primary care; and poor information about health. The country spends much more than other peer economies on healthcare, but too little and too ineffectively on public health (here is a reference to my own work on this: https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/pandp.20231000). Could we, and should we, divert some of the massive investments in AI towards public health if what we want is better health and longer lives for Americans? (And yes, new drugs will benefit citizens of other countries as well, but they can also invest more in public health and the bulk of global AI spending is currently in the United States).

  • 23:40 · @pstAsiatech Paul Triolothread · 3 posts推文串 · 3 条
    A quick thread on this latest story on diversion of GPUs to China. 🧵 It is an issue increasingly OBeD and outdated by the developments of the past 6 months, as we have enterted the Age of Agentic Super Intelligence.post ↗原帖 ↗
    @pstAsiatechThe latest diversion story.... Money quote: “US prosecutors allege less than one half of one percent of Nvidia products have been diverted.” @pstAsiatech Repeats a lot of the same old discredited narratives and breaks no new ground https://www.bloomberg.com/news/features/2026-10-01/nvidia-faces-questions-over-china-ai-chip-smuggling-cases?utm_source=website&utm_medium=share&utm_campaign=twitter
    It slides from legitimate compliance questions into an undefined obligation for Nvidia to implement a broader anti-China strategy. Allegedly fake leases, insufficient facilities, and possible employee involvement warrant serious investigation. Existing BIS guidance already requires companies to investigate suspicious circumstances and prohibits deliberate avoidance of relevant information. But the quoted criticism of Nvidia for following the law introduces a different standard: that a company should restrict otherwise permitted business because China is a geopolitical adversary. The article should distinguish failures to satisfy existing compliance duties from demands that Nvidia voluntarily exceed government policy. It also needs clearer attribution among Nvidia, server manufacturers, distributors, cloud operators, individual employees, and BIS. An employee’s alleged participation is serious; it does not by itself establish corporate authorization or knowledge. As I have noted, supply chains involving GPUs are very complicated, there is no real acknowedgment of this here. This Substack should be a must read for anyone writing on the topic, industry sources told me it was the best thing they have read on this complex topic, more than a year ago: https://pstaidecrypted.substack.com/p/us-government-concern-over-gpu-diversionspost ↗原帖 ↗
    And finally, the reporting conflates evidence of leakage with evidence of overall policy failure—and commercial AI competition with demonstrated military harm. Documented smuggling can establish enforcement weaknesses. It cannot, without a counterfactual, establish how much export controls have reduced access, raised costs, delayed deployment, or encouraged substitution. Conversely, China’s continued model progress does not establish that controls have had no effect. The report also moves from military procurement examples to commercial labs’ benchmark performance and then to a sweeping national-security narrative without tracing the intervening links. The vast majority of the uses cases in China for all of the private sector company access to domestic, diverted, or remotely accessed GPUs are civilian! Not military, let's at least acknowledge this reality, instead of making vague allusions to all of this activity somehow contributing to China's military capabilities. The discussion of Huang’s relationship with Trump and their AI-safety rhetoric adds political context, but supplies little evidence about particular diversion transactions and is really irrelevant to the topic at hand. The critical missing analysis is what the leakage changes strategically, relative to unrestricted access—and whether proposed remedies would materially reduce it at an acceptable cost. Along with what I believe is the real issue, how does the US collaborate with China on AI safety, and how can Chinese AI companies test advanced models without access to sufficient compute? That is the real national security question going forward, not bean counting of diverted GPUs without any sense of how they are being used and what difference they make. End Thread.post ↗原帖 ↗

    I amplified the IIF's number on the change in the maturity structure of issuance that Wolf used, and it doesn't seem to check out. That said, I do think net bill issuance is poised to jump again -- the 4qs to q2 2026 are a temporary lowmedia带图post ↗原帖 ↗
    @DavidZervosDCIn the @ftopinion article below, @martinwolf_ claims that the average maturity of US Treasury issuance decreased from 6.1 years in 2024 to 2.5 years in 2026. He should check his math. The 2024 figure appears to entirely exclude bill issuance in that year, whereas bill issuance is fully included for 2026.  In effect, he is comparing apples to broccoli.   The truth is that the WAM of all outstanding debt has barely changed between 2024 and today (5.9 vs 5.8 years).  The Treasury regularly publishes a wide range of portfolio metrics in order to provide market participants with an accurate picture of the debt maturity structure. @FT writers should consult these data before publishing claims that mislead its readers.

    "Traditional Chinese wisdom holds that “the sage attends to matters early,” emphasizing the ability to infer the whole from subtle signs and prevent trouble before it occurs. This is inherently consistent with a prevention-first approach to governance. There are no ready-made standard answers to the ethical questions of the digital-intelligence era, but the answers always remain in human hands. Only by remaining people-centered, putting prevention first, pursuing collaborative governance, and guiding AI to act for good can we ensure that digital and intelligent technologies truly become “intelligence for good” that enhances human well-being. 中华传统智慧讲究“圣人早从事焉”，强调见微知著、防患未然，这与预防为先的治理思路内在相通。数智时代的伦理之问没有现成标准答案，但答案始终掌握在人类手中。坚持以人为本、预防为先、协同共治，引导人工智能向善而行，才能让数智技术真正成为增进人类福祉的“善智”。"post ↗原帖 ↗

  • 04:47 · @niubi Bill Bishop 利明璋
    "Some insist that "humans are the creators of AI, so no matter how powerful it becomes, humans will certainly be able to control it," and advocate "development first, let the bullets fly for a while." (让子弹飞一会儿, "Let the Bullets Fly," a catchphrase from Jiang Wen's 2010 film of that name, meaning wait and see how things play out.) This pattern of pursuing rapid iteration, with "capabilities sprinting while safety walks slowly," closely resembles the Soviet nuclear industry's model of "emphasizing construction and neglecting safety" before the Chernobyl accident. Others believe that "simply giving AI a one-click shutdown will solve every problem." Little do they know that when the Chernobyl reactor went out of control, the operators pressed precisely the AZ-5 emergency shutdown button, but because of a design flaw in the control rods, in its first moments this "brake" instead became an "accelerator," directly intensifying the explosion. The "stop button" that humans think they are holding may be exactly what accelerates the loss of control. 有些观点坚持认为“人类是人工智能的创建者，再强大也肯定能够被人类控制”，并且提出“发展优先，让子弹飞一会儿”。这种追求快速迭代、“能力快跑、安全慢走”的格局，与切尔诺贝利事故发生前苏联核工业“重建设、轻安全”的模式高度相似。还有观点认为“只要为AI设置一键关停就可以解决所有问题”。殊不知，切尔诺贝利操作员在反应堆失控时按下的正是紧急停堆按钮AZ-5，却因控制棒设计缺陷，这一“刹车”在最初瞬间反而变成了“加油门”，直接加剧了爆炸。人类以为握在手里的“停止键”，可能恰恰是加速失控的关键。 Therefore, if the world continues to dodge risks with technological optimism, shelve governance in favor of competition, and cover up problems through informational secrecy, then AI's "Chernobyl moment" may not be a question of "whether it will happen" but of "when it will happen." 因此，如果全球再继续以技术乐观主义回避风险、以竞争优先搁置治理、以信息封闭掩盖问题，那么AI的“切尔诺贝利时刻”或许不是“是否会发生”，而是“何时会发生”。"post ↗原帖 ↗
    @niubiGuarding Against the Risk of Frontier AI Technology Spinning Out of Control 防范前沿人工智能技术失控风险 - Zeng Yi 曾毅, Wu Yuzhang Chair Professor at the Gaoling School of Artificial Intelligence, Renmin University of China, and Dean of the Beijing Institute of AI Safety and Governance https://sinocism.notion.site/Guarding-Against-the-Risk-of-Frontier-AI-Technology-Spinning-Out-of-Control-Zeng-Yi-3ec84ece41d781ba8caee886703dbb21 Even as AI is greatly driving social progress, real safety concerns have emerged along with it: is the artificial intelligence we are pouring our efforts into building clean hydropower that benefits humanity, or another "reactor" harboring hidden risks? Its destructive power comes not from nuclear fission but from intelligence itself. When these "seemingly intelligent" information systems begin to make decisions on their own, act on their own, and even keep breaking through the boundaries humans have set, will we replay the tragedy of the Chernobyl engineers, who went on firmly believing the technology was still under control even as the crisis unfolded? It should be recognized that AI's so-called "Chernobyl moment" is a metaphor for risk warning, not a prediction that disaster has already occurred or is imminent. But the potential systemic loss-of-control risk it points to is no longer confined to science fiction. 当前，AI在极大推动社会进步的同时，现实的安全隐忧也随之浮现：我们倾力构建的人工智能，究竟是造福人类的清洁水电，还是又一座潜藏风险的“反应堆”？它的破坏力并非来自核裂变，而是源于智能本身。当这“看似智能”的信息系统开始自主决策、自主行动，甚至不断自主冲破人类预设的边界，我们会不会重演切尔诺贝利工程师的悲剧——危机发生时却依旧笃信技术仍在掌控之中？应当看到，所谓AI的“切尔诺贝利时刻”，是风险警示的隐喻，并非预判灾难已经发生或即刻降临，但它所指向的可能出现的系统性失控风险，早已不再局限于科幻想象。

    The new Fuzhou-Xiamen 350 km/hr line offloaded capacity from the original 250 km/hr line. This new line has ramped up to 60-80 daily train pairs. https://x.com/GlennLuk/status/1708016653219529030post ↗原帖 ↗
    @GlennLukThe new Fuzhou-Xiamen line also connected two major cities already served by HSR, albeit more directly (featuring three cross-sea bridges) allowing for higher speeds (350 vs 250 km/hr) — shaving about a third of the time off the journey.
    This has relieved congestion on the original line (which opened in 2010), which had been one of the most capacity-constrained lines in China's network. Passenger traffic has also shifted to more regional commuter-type traffic and lower-cost D trains. One thing I also learned recently is that this line was another dual-use (passenger/freight) line and is now able to accommodate more freight along one of China's most industrialized corridors.post ↗原帖 ↗
